Security-polymorphic process definitions.

Let us examine the pattern checks
\sim_{\langle\psi,\mathbf{guest}\rangle}
invoked by
\mathbf{case}\,y_{1}(\dots)
in
\mathbf{Bank}_{A}
,
relating the branches corresponding to black and white tokens.
The sends along
y_{1}
match in both branches, as demanded by
SndLab
(since
\Psi\not\Vdash\psi\,{\sqsubseteq}\,\mathbf{guest}
),
even though the sent labels are not the same.
The unsynchronized spawn and send along
w_{1}
is verified by
Unsync-Spawn
1
and
Unsync
1
, resp., since
\Psi\Vdash\psi\sqsubseteq\psi
.
The matching tail calls are verified with
Sync-Spawn
.

6
Progress-sensitive noninterference

This section presents our main result, PSNI, which we prove using a logical relation.

6.1
Attacker model

The attacker model assumes a configuration
\mathcal{D}
with prior annotation of its free channels with security levels, the attacker’s confidentiality level
\xi
, and a nondeterministic scheduler. The attacker knows the source code of
\mathcal{D}
, can only observe the messages sent along the free channels of
\mathcal{D}
with confidentiality level
c\sqsubseteq\xi
, and cannot measure the passing of time.

6.2
Noninterference via an integrity logical relation

Noninterference amounts to a process
equivalence up to the confidentiality level
\xi
of an observer.
In a message-passing system, it boils down to an equivalence of a configuration with interacting processes.
This section focuses on noninterference for tree-shaped configurations. The definition can be extended to forests by enforcing pairwise relation between their trees.

An open configuration
\Psi_{0};\Delta\Vdash\mathcal{D}::x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle
has the free channels
\Delta
and
x_{\alpha}
to communicate with its external environment; it sends outgoing messages to and receives incoming messages from the environment along these free channels.
Two observationally equivalent configurations may only differ in outgoing messages of confidentiality level
c_{o}\not\sqsupseteq\xi
, assuming that the incoming messages of confidentiality level
c_{i}\sqsubseteq\xi
are the same.
We introduce a
logical relation
that captures this idea and accounts for integrity and regrading policies.

The logical relation relates two open configurations
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
—the two runs of the program under consideration—and asserts that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
send related messages to the environment, if they receive related messages from the environment.
The term interpretation of the logical relation, defined in
Fig.

20
, allows the first configuration
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
to step internally until the configuration is ready to send or receive a message across at least one external channel. Then, it requires the second configuration
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
to step internally so that the resulting configurations are in the value interpretation of the logical relation, defined in
Fig.

19
and
Fig.

18
. We call the external channels, e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K}
in
Fig.

20
, the interface of
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
. The metavariable
K
stands for either
x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle
or simply
\_{:}1\langle\top,\top\rangle
which refers to an arbitrary unobservable channel.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}({\mathcal{B}_{1}},{\mathcal{B}_{2}})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta\Vdash K\rrbracket^{m+1}&\mathsf{iff}&(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathsf{Tree}_{\Psi_{0}}(\Delta\Vdash K)\,\mathsf{and}\forall\,\Upsilon_{1},\,\Theta_{1},\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}.\,\mathsf{if}\;\mathcal{D}_{1}\mapsto^{{*}_{\Upsilon_{1};\Theta_{1}}}\;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}\,\mathsf{then}\\
&&\exists\,\Upsilon_{2}\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}.\,\mathsf{\,such\,that\,}\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\mapsto^{{*}_{\Upsilon_{2}}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}\;\mathsf{and}\;\Upsilon_{1}\subseteq\Upsilon_{2}\,\mathsf{and}\\
&&\forall\,y_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{Out}(\Delta\Vdash K).\,\mathbf{if}\,y_{\alpha}\in\Upsilon_{1}.\,\mathbf{then}\,({\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}};{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta\Vdash K\rrbracket_{\cdot;y_{\alpha}}^{m+1}\,\mathsf{and}\\[2.0pt]
&&\forall\,y_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{In}(\Delta\Vdash K).\mathbf{if}\,y_{\alpha}\in\Theta_{1}.\,\mathbf{then}\,({\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}};{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta\Vdash K\rrbracket_{y_{\alpha};\cdot}^{m+1}\\[4.0pt]
({\mathcal{B}_{1}},{\mathcal{B}_{2}})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta\Vdash K\rrbracket^{0}&\mathsf{iff}&(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathsf{Tree}_{\Psi_{0}}(\Delta\Vdash K)\end{array}

Figure 12:
Value interpretation of logical relation for
right
communications.

The idea is to build an interface consisting of those external channels of the configurations that may impact the attacker’s observations. As such, not only do we need to include the observable channels, i.e., with confidentiality level
c\sqsubseteq\xi
, in the interface, but also those with higher integrity than the observer, i.e., with integrity level
e\sqsubseteq\xi
. After all, if a channel’s integrity is high enough (and thus its level is low), the messages along it may affect an observable outcome via synchronization patterns.
We call such an interface
integrity interface
since low-confidentiality channels are all high-integrity by typing.

To build an
integrity interface
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
, we close off their external low-integrity (
e\not\sqsubseteq\xi
) channels on the left by composing the channels with any well-typed provider and on the right with any well-typed client. We may use different low-integrity clients and providers to compose with each program run. These clients/providers can send different and unsynchronized messages along their high-confidentiality and low-integrity connections to
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
. The term interpretation is designed to ensure that well-typed configurations do not leak these different messages to the attacker.
Fig.

13
defines an equivalence relation between two configurations based on this idea: it composes them with low-integrity providers/clients and calls the term interpretation symmetrically on the compositions.
In the definition, we use the projection function to build the integrity interface, e.g.,
\Delta{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}}\xi
projects out the channels
y_{\beta}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\in\Delta
with
\xi\not\sqsubseteq e
.
The predicate
\mathcal{D}_{1}\in\mathsf{Tree}_{\Psi_{0}}({\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K})
indicates that the configuration
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
is well-typed.
In the term and value interpretations, we generalize this predicate to the binary case,
(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathsf{Tree}_{\Psi_{0}}({\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K})
indicating that both
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
are of the same type.

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\Delta_{1}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{1}::x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle)\equiv^{\Psi_{0}}_{\xi}(\Delta_{2}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{2}::y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle)\;\;\mathsf{iff}\\
\;\;{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\in\mathsf{Tree}({\Delta_{1}}\Vdash x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle)\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,{\mathcal{D}_{2}}\in\mathsf{Tree}({\Delta_{2}}\Vdash y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle)\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\Delta_{1}{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}}\xi=\Delta_{2}{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}}\xi=\Delta\,\,\mathsf{and}\\
\;\;x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi=y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi=K\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\forall\,\mathcal{B}_{1}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta_{1}).\,\forall\,\mathcal{B}_{2}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta_{2}).\\
\;\;\forall\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle).\,\forall\mathcal{T}_{2}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle).\\
\;\;\forall\,m.\,(\mathcal{B}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket{\Delta}\Vdash{K}\rrbracket^{m},\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\forall\,m.\,(\mathcal{B}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{1})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket{\Delta}\Vdash{K}\rrbracket^{m}.\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}\cdot\,\,\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\cdot)\\
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta,x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\,\,\;\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{T}\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta)\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{Tree}(\cdot\Vdash x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle),\mathbf{or}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{B}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta)\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{T}\in\mathbf{L\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\,\,\;\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{Tree}(x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\Vdash\_:1\langle\top,\top\rangle),\,\,\mathbf{or}\,\,e\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{T}=\cdot\end{array}

Figure 13:
Logical equivalence.

The term interpretation allows stepping configuration
\mathcal{D}_{1}\mapsto^{*_{\Upsilon_{1};\Theta_{1}}}{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}

by iterated application of the rewriting rules defined in
Fig.

8
.
The star expresses that zero to multiple internal steps can be taken.
The superscripts
\Upsilon_{1};\Theta_{1}
denote two sets of channels occurring in the interface
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K}
.
The set
\Theta_{1}
collects the
incoming
channels, i.e., channels that a process in
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
is ready to receive from, and the set
\Upsilon_{1}
collects the
outgoing
channels, i.e., channels with a message in
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
ready to be sent.
Assuming that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
steps to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
, generating the outgoing channels
\Upsilon_{1}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
must be stepped
\mathcal{D}_{2}\mapsto^{*_{\Upsilon_{2}}}{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}}
to produce at least the same set of outgoing channels, i.e., the set
\Upsilon_{2}
such that
\Upsilon_{1}\subseteq\Upsilon_{2}
.
The term interpretation then calls the value interpretation on the resulting configurations
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\prime}

for every
channel that has a message ready for transmission in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
, and thus
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
for every
channel that has a process waiting for a message in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
.
Insisting on
\Upsilon_{2}
being a superset of
\Upsilon_{1}
ensures progress-sensitive noninterference without timing attacks:
if a configuration produces observable messages along a set of channels, the other configuration has to be able to produce the equivalent set of messages with zero or some internal steps.
The term interpretation uses focus channels as a subscript to the value interpretation to support simultaneous communications — when there are multiple messages ready to be sent or received along channels in the interface.
The subscript
\cdot;y_{\alpha}
indicates that
y_{\alpha}\in\Upsilon_{1}
and
y_{\alpha};\cdot
that
y_{\alpha}\in\Theta_{1}
.

The value interpretation accounts for every message sent from or received by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
,
amounting to two cases per connective: one for a message exchanged along a channel in
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K}
and one for a message exchanged along a channel in
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}
.
We refer to the former as communications to the
right
(
Fig.

18
) and the latter as communications to the
left
(
Fig.

19
).
The value interpretation generally establishes the following pattern: it asserts relatedness of outgoing messages, but assumes relatedness of incoming messages.
For example,
\&
on the left (
l_{3}
in
Fig.

19
)
asserts
the sending of related messages and pushes the messages into the environment, yielding
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\prime}
.
Now,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\prime}
, can each step internally, e.g., to consume the incoming messages, requiring them to be in the term interpretation.
On the other hand,
\&

on the right (
r_{3}
in
Fig.

18
)
assumes
receipt of related messages and pushes the messages into the configurations
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
.

Relatedness
for messages is determined by how they can impact the attacker’s observations. If their carrier channel is observable to the attacker, i.e., has confidentiality level
c\sqsubseteq\xi
, then related messages must have the same labels. But if the channel only affects the attacker’s observations via synchronization patterns, related messages may have different labels. The clause
c\sqsubseteq\xi\rightarrow k_{1}=k_{2}
in the value interpretation conveys this, enforcing equality of the communicated labels only if the channel is observable.

Relatedness for higher-order types (
\otimes
and
\multimap
) is a bit more subtle.
In particular, it requires future observations along the exchanged channels to be related.
For example,
l_{5}
in
Fig.

19
for
\multimap
-left asserts
existence of a message
\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}x_{\beta}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\,y_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle})

and of subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}

in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
.
The clause comprises two invocations of the term relation,

(\mathcal{T}_{1};\mathcal{T}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta^{\prime}\Vdash x_{\beta}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\rrbracket^{m}
,
asserting that future observations to be made along the sent channel
x_{\beta}
are related, and

(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{1};\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta^{\prime\prime},y_{\alpha+1}{:}B\langle c,e\rangle\Vdash K\rrbracket^{m}
,
asserting that the continuations
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{2}}
are related.
Conversely,
r_{5}
in
Fig.

18
for
\multimap
-right assumes
receipt of a message
\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}x_{\beta}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\,y_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle})
and invokes the term relation

(\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\,y_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle});\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\,y_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle}))\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket\Delta,x_{\beta}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\Vdash y_{\alpha+1}{:}B\langle c,e\rangle\rrbracket^{m}
.

As we support general recursive types, we need an index to stratify our logical relation
[
3
,
5
]
.
We tie our index to the number of
observations
that can be made along the interface
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K}
, as suggested in
[
7
]
.
We thus bound the value and term interpretation of our logical relation by the number of observations
m
, for
m\geq 0
, and attach them as superscripts to the relation’s interface
{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}\Delta}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0898,0.4258,0.0859}K}
.
The base case of the term interpretation, i.e.,
m=0
, is a trivial relation.

The fundamental theorem states that any well-typed
\mathsf{SINTEGRITY}
configuration is equivalent to itself up to the level of an arbitrary observer.

Theorem 1

(Fundamental theorem)
.

For all security levels
\xi
, and a well-typed configuration
{\Psi_{0};\Delta\Vdash\mathcal{D}::u_{\alpha}{:}T\langle c,e\rangle}
we have
(\Delta\Vdash\mathcal{D}::u_{\alpha}{:}T\langle c,e\rangle)\equiv^{\Psi_{0}}_{\xi}(\Delta\Vdash\mathcal{D}::u_{\alpha}{:}T\langle c,e\rangle).

Proof.

We present a proof sketch; see the appendix for details.
By the definition of logical equivalence (
Fig.

13
), we first need to close off the external low integrity (
e\not\sqsubseteq\xi
) channels in
\Delta
and
u_{\alpha}{:}T\langle c,e\rangle
by composing
\mathcal{D}
with arbitrary well-typed providers and clients, resp. We use low integrity clients,
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, and low-integrity providers,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, to compose with each run, resulting in two configurations,
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{B}_{1}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\mathcal{B}_{2}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
Configurations
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are both well-typed for the integrity interface:
{\Psi_{0};\Delta^{\prime}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{i}::K}

where
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}}\xi
and
K=u_{\alpha}{:}T\langle c,e\rangle{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}}\xi
. By the definition in
Fig.

13
, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are in the term interpretation with the integrity interface, i.e.,
\forall\,m.\,(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket{\Delta^{\prime}}\Vdash{K}\rrbracket^{m}
and
\forall\,m.\,(\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1})\in\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{\Psi_{0}}\llbracket{\Delta^{\prime}}\Vdash{K}\rrbracket^{m}
.
We prove the former by induction on
m
; the proof of the latter is symmetric.
Specifically, we prove a more general theorem (
Thm.

24
in the appendix) for any
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
with the same observable outcome, using the notion of relevant nodes (
Def.

11
in the appendix).
By the definition of the term interpretation in
Fig.

20
, the base case (
m=0
) is straightforward.
For the inductive case, following the first row of
Fig.

20
,
we assume arbitrary
\Upsilon_{1}
,
\Theta_{1}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
such that

\mathcal{D}_{1}\mapsto^{{*}_{\Upsilon_{1};\Theta_{1}}}\;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
. We apply a lemma (
Lem.

14
in the appendix) stating that
\mathcal{D}_{2}
can simulate the internal steps taken by
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, producing at least the same set of outgoing channels
\Upsilon_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}_{2}\mapsto^{{*}_{\Upsilon_{2}}}\;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
, such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
continue to have the same observable outcomes.
Finally, for every channel
x_{\alpha}
in
\Upsilon_{1}
and
\Theta_{1}
, we case analyze on the type of
x_{\alpha}
, showing that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
are in the value interpretation, with
x_{\alpha}
being the focus channel. To do so, we use the induction hypothesis to establish that after the corresponding communication with the environment, the continuations of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
are related by the term interpretation for a smaller index.

∎

6.3
Adequacy

Next, we prove an adequacy theorem showing that two logically equivalent configurations are
bisimilar
up to observations of confidentiality
\xi
.

For adequacy, we are interested in
a confidentiality interface
, i.e., channels with observable max confidentiality
c\sqsubseteq\xi
; after all, our goal is to prove that the configurations are equivalent up to the confidentiality of an observer.
Because the integrity interface of our logical relation is a
superset
of the confidentiality interface, we need to close off those channels in the integrity interface that are of high-confidentiality (
c\not\sqsubseteq\xi
).
Note that these high-confidentiality channels are of high-integrity (
e\sqsubseteq\xi
).
To close off these channels, we compose the open configurations with high-confidentiality clients and providers, possibly different ones for each program run.
These high-integrity clients and providers are connected to the open configurations via high-integrity channels and,
as a result, may affect the observable outcome of the two runs via synchronization patterns.
We therefore require them to be logically equivalent.

Based on this idea,
Fig.

14
defines the
bisimulation up to
confidentiality
\xi
denoted as
\approx_{a}^{\xi}
, for two well-typed configurations: it first composes the two configurations with high-confidentiality providers (
\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}
) and clients (
\mathbf{H\text{-}CClient}
), while insisting that the high-integrity parts of the providers and clients are logically equivalent (using the relations
\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}
and
\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IClient}
). Then it invokes an asynchronous bisimulation
\approx_{a}
on the compositions.
The definition uses a projection function
{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}}\xi
to build the confidentiality interface, e.g.,
\Delta{\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}}\xi
projects out the channels in
\Delta
with confidentiality
c\not\sqsubseteq\xi
.

The
asynchronous bisimulation

\approx_{a}
invoked by the definition in
Fig.

14
uses a labeled transition system (LTS) following the standard definition of asynchronous bisimulation
[
41
]
. The relation
\mathcal{D}_{1}\approx_{a}\mathcal{D}_{2}
states that every internal step or external action of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
can be (weakly) simulated by
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and vice-versa. For example, when
\mathcal{D}_{1}
takes an action by sending output
q
via an external channel
x_{\alpha}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{x_{\alpha}}\,q}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
, the bisimulation ensures that for some
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
, we have
\mathcal{D}_{2}\xRightarrow{\overline{x_{\alpha}}\,q}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}\approx_{a}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}
.
Here,
\xRightarrow{\overline{x_{\alpha}}\,q}
stands for taking zero or more internal steps before outputting
q
along
x_{\alpha}
. The full definition of bisimulation is in the appendix.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{1}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{1}::x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle\approx^{\xi}_{a}\Delta_{2}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{2}::y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle\,\,\mathsf{iff}\\
\,\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\in\mathsf{Tree}({\Delta_{1}}\Vdash x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle)\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\in\mathsf{Tree}({\Delta_{2}}\Vdash y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle)\,\mathsf{and}\\
\,\,\Delta=\Delta_{1}\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}\xi=\Delta_{2}\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}\xi\,\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\,K=y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}\xi=x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{cf}}\xi\,\,\,\mathsf{and}\\
\,\,\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi=\Delta_{2}\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi\,\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\,K^{\prime}=y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi=x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle\Downarrow^{\mathsf{ig}}\xi\,\,\,\mathsf{and}\\
\,\,\forall\mathcal{B}_{1}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta_{1}),\mathcal{B}_{2}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta_{2}).\\
\,\,\forall\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CClient}^{\xi}(x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle),\,\mathcal{T}_{2}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CClient}^{\xi}(y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle).\\
\,\,\mathsf{if}(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta^{\prime}\backslash\Delta)\,\mathsf{and}\,(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(K^{\prime}\backslash K)\,\mathsf{then}\,\mathcal{B}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{1}\approx_{a}\mathcal{B}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{2}.\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}\cdot\,\,\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\cdot)\\
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta,x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\,\,\;\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994ptc\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{T}\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta)\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{Tree}(\cdot\Vdash x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle),\mathbf{or}\\
\hskip 9.24994ptc\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{B}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta)\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{T}\in\mathbf{H\text{-}CClient}^{\xi}(x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\,\,\,\;\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994ptc\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{Tree}(x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\Vdash\_:1\langle\top,\top\rangle),\mathbf{or}\\
\hskip 9.24994ptc\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{T}=\cdot\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\cdot,\cdot)\,\,\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\cdot)\\
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta,x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\;\;\,\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,\mathcal{B}_{i}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta),\mathbf{or}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\mathcal{B}_{i}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IProvider}^{\xi}(\Delta)\,\,\mathsf{and}\,\,\cdot\Vdash\mathcal{T}_{1}\equiv_{\xi}^{\Psi_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{2}::x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle.\\[5.0pt]
(\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(\_\langle\top,\top\rangle)\\
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})\in\mathbf{Hrel\text{-}IClient}^{\xi}(x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle)\;\;\,\mathsf{iff}\\
\hskip 9.24994pte\not\sqsubseteq\xi\,\,\mathbf{or}\,\,e\sqsubseteq\xi\,\mathsf{and}\,x_{\alpha}{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\Vdash\mathcal{T}_{1}\equiv_{\xi}^{\Psi_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{2}::\_:1\langle\top,\top\rangle\end{array}

Figure 14:
Asynchronous bisimulation up to observations of confidentiality
\xi
.

Now we are ready to present our adequacy theorem stating that, given an observer level
\xi
, logically equivalent configurations are bisimilar up to observations of confidentiality
\xi
.
The proof of the theorem relies on a compositionality lemma, which ensures a harmony between asserts and assumes in the value-interpretation of the logical relation.

Lemma 2

(Compositionality)
.

\forall m.\,(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi_{0}}^{\xi}\llbracket\Delta,u_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle}{:}T\Vdash K\rrbracket^{m}\,\mathsf{and}\,\forall m.\,(\mathcal{T}_{1};\mathcal{T}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi_{0}}^{\xi}\llbracket\Delta^{\prime}\Vdash u_{\alpha}^{\langle c,e\rangle}{:}T\rrbracket^{m}\,\,
if and only if

\forall k.\,(\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{T}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi_{0}}^{\xi}\llbracket\Delta^{\prime},\Delta\Vdash K\rrbracket^{k}.

Theorem 3

(Adequacy)
.

If

(\Delta_{1}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{1}::x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle)\equiv^{\Psi_{0}}_{\xi}(\Delta_{2}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{2}::y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle)
then
(\Delta_{1}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{1}::x_{\alpha}{:}A_{1}\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle)\approx_{a}^{\xi}(\Delta_{2}\Vdash\mathcal{D}_{2}::y_{\beta}{:}A_{2}\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle)
.

Proof.

Recall from
Fig.

14
that
\approx_{a}^{\xi}
composes
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
with arbitrary high-confidentiality (
c\not\sqsubseteq\xi
) clients and providers, building a confidentiality interface
\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket
.
Let us call the high-confidentiality providers
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
and the high-confidentiality clients
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
We can partition the providers into high-integrity and low-integrity parts to get
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{i}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{i}
(similarly for the clients
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{i}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{i}
), where superscripts
\mathit{HI}
and
\mathit{LI}
correspond to high-integrity and low-integrtiy parts, resp.
Our goal is to prove
\mathcal{B}^{HI}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{LI}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{HI}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{LI}_{1}\approx_{a}\mathcal{B}^{HI}_{2}\mathcal{B}^{LI}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{HI}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{LI}_{2}
.

Step 1.

The first step is to show that the two compositions are related by the term interpretation as well, i.e.,
\forall m.(\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1};\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket
.
To do so, we can use the definition from
Fig.

13
for
\equiv^{\Psi_{0}}_{\xi}
to compose
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
with given low integrity clients and providers
\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}
to build the integrity interface
\llbracket\Delta^{i}\Vdash K^{i}\rrbracket
and get
\forall m.(\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1};\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket\Delta^{i}\Vdash K^{i}\rrbracket
.
However, this is not enough to achieve our goal as the relation pertains to the integrity interface, and thus, the composition only includes the low integrity providers/clients.
To build the confidentiality interface and include the high integrity parts, we use the fact that the high integrity providers
\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}
(and clients
\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}
) are themselves logically equivalent.
We use our compositionality lemma (
Lem.

2
) to compose the high-integrity channels in the integrity interface with these providers/clients and show that the composition results in two logically equivalent configurations, i.e.,
\forall m.(\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1};\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket
.

Step 2.
We complete the proof by connecting our logically related configurations to an observational equivalence relation for session types
[
7
]
, which is proved sound and complete for asynchronous bisimulation.
We first show that our integrity term interpretation implies the observational equivalence relation in
[
7
]
when we consider a confidentiality interface, and then use their soundness result to show that the integrity term interpretation
\forall m.(\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{1};\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{B}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{HI}}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\mathit{LI}}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket
implies bisimilarity
\mathcal{B}^{HI}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{LI}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{HI}_{1}\mathcal{T}^{LI}_{1}\approx_{a}\mathcal{B}^{HI}_{2}\mathcal{B}^{LI}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{HI}_{2}\mathcal{T}^{LI}_{2}
.

Next, we briefly explain how our integrity logical relation coincides with the observational equivalence relation (for well-typed configurations) in
[
7
]
when considering a confidentiality interface.
The observational equivalence in
[
7
]
is defined via a logical relation similar to the one developed in this paper, but only considering the confidentiality interface. Let us call the term and value interpretations of our logical relation
\mathcal{E}^{i}
and
\mathcal{V}^{i}
(defined in
Figs.

20
,
19
and
18
) and the ones defined in
[
7
]

\mathcal{E}^{c}
and
\mathcal{V}^{c}
, resp.
The relation
\mathcal{E}^{i}
is invoked for the integrity interface
\llbracket\Delta^{i}\Vdash K^{i}\rrbracket
in
Fig.

13
, and similarly
\mathcal{E}^{c}
is invoked for the confidentiality interface
\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket
, where, by definition,
\Delta^{c}
is a subset of (or equal to)
\Delta^{i}
and
K^{c}
is a subset of (or equal to)
K^{i}
.
As the integrity logical relation may contain non-observable channels (
c\not\sqsubseteq\xi
), it only insists that the same labels are sent when communication is along observable channels.
Concretely,
\mathcal{V}^{i}
in lines
(l_{2})
,
(l_{3})
,
(r_{2})
, and
(r_{3})
only insists that the labels
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
sent/received along a channel are the same if
c\sqsubseteq\xi
.
However,
\mathcal{V}^{c}
always enforces sending the same labels, since a priori the condition
c\sqsubseteq\xi
holds for all the channels in its interface.
In all other regards,
\mathcal{E}^{i}
and
\mathcal{V}^{i}
have the same definition as
\mathcal{E}^{c}
and
\mathcal{V}^{c}
.
As a result, it is straightforward to observe that given an interface
\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}
with only observable channels (channels with
c\sqsubseteq\xi
), we have
\forall m.\,(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{i}\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket^{m}
iff
\forall m.\,(\mathcal{D}_{1};\mathcal{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{E}^{c}\llbracket\Delta^{c}\Vdash K^{c}\rrbracket^{m}
.
∎

7
Related work

IFC type systems using linearity.

Conceptually most closely related to our work is the work by Zdancewic and Myers
on
ordered linear continuations

[
50
,
51
]
.
The authors consider continuation-passing style (CPS) security-typed languages
to verify noninterference not only for source-level programs but also compiled code.
The authors observe that the possibility to lower the
pc
label upon exiting control flow constructs,
present in imperative source-level languages,
is no longer available in a CPS language.
To rectify the loss of flexibility they introduce ordered linear continuations.
Similar to our pattern checks, ordered linear continuations allow downgrading of the
pc
label after branching on high,
because linearity enforces the continuations to be used in every branch, in the order prescribed.
In contrast to our work, the authors only consider a sequential language and only prove PINI.
Our work moreover establishes the connection to integrity,
facilitating regrading policies that are polymorphic in the security lattice for ultimate flexibility.

In another line of work Zdancewic and Myers again employ linearity
for increased flexibility and a stronger noninterference statement
[
52
]
.
The authors consider a concurrent language with a store and first-class channels.
Their main focus is observational determinism, ensuring immunity to internal timing attacks and
attacks that exploit information about thread scheduling.
To this end the authors introduce linear channels and a race freedom analysis.
Given that
\mathsf{SINTEGRITY}
enjoys confluence, like other linear session type languages,
it rules out timing attacks that exploit the relative order of messages,
which seems to be a stronger property than immunity to internal timing attacks considered by the authors.
Moreover, we establish PSNI for
\mathsf{SINTEGRITY}
, whereas the authors only prove PINI.

IFC session type systems.

In terms of underlying language, the work most closely related to ours is the one by Derakhshan et al.
[
23
,
7
]
.
The authors develop an IFC type system for the same family of linear session types but only consider confidentiality.
Their system annotates the process term judgments with running and max confidentiality.
Their typing rules only ensure that the running confidentiality (aka taint level) is updated correctly after each receive and that a tainted process does not leak information via send.
In particular, the rules do not allow decreasing the taint level at any point.
As a result the authors’ type system suffers from the same restrictiveness as other IFC type systems for concurrent languages,
requiring each loop iteration to run at the maximal confidentiality reached throughout an arbitrary iteration.
For example, the authors’ IFC type system rejects the banking example in
§​

5.4
: as soon as the bank receives a message from one customer, say Alice, it will be tainted and cannot send a message to any other customer, say Bob.
In fact, the authors’ IFC type system rejects all well-typed examples presented in this paper even though they enjoy PSNI.
We make our IFC type system more
flexible
by designing synchronization policies to enable regrading of the taint level and using integrity labels to make the policies composable, both of which are novel to our system.
Designing these composable policies was an intricate task, particularly due to dealing with both concurrency and general recursion.

Our logical relation for integrity is inspired by Balzer et al.’s
[
7
]
logical relation for equivalence.
The logical relation for equivalence is defined based on the confidentiality interface. Our logical relation, however, is based on the larger integrity interface to enable the proof of the fundamental theorem.
We prove our adequacy theorem by proving compositionality for our logical relation,
which then allows us to recast our logical relation in terms of the logical relation for equivalence by the authors,
delivering adequacy as a corollary.

IFC type systems for multiparty session types and process calculi.

IFC type systems have also been explored for multiparty session types

[
13
,
11
,
14
,
12
]
.
These works explore declassification
[
13
,
11
]
and
flexible runtime monitoring techniques
[
14
,
12
]
.
Our work not only differs in use of session type paradigm (i.e., binary vs. multiparty)
but also in use of a logical relation for showing noninterference.
Our work is more distantly related with IFC type systems for process calculi

[
30
,
31
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
27
,
27
,
33
,
52
,
37
]
.
These works prevent information leakage by associating a security label with channels/types/actions
[
31
]
, read/write policies with channels
[
27
,
27
]
, or capabilities with expressions
[
17
]
.
Honda et al.
[
31
]
also use a substructural type system and
prove a sound embedding of Dependency Core Calculus (DCC)
[
2
]
into their calculus.
Our work sets itself apart in its use of session types and meta theoretic developments based on logical relations.
Moreover, our IFC type system
is more permissive as it allows for regrading of the taint level, while preserving noninterference.

Declassification.

Our notion of regrading may seem related to declassification,
which has extensively been studied for IFC type systems for functional and imperative languages

[
24
,
1
,
35
,
49
,
53
,
48
,
36
,
16
,
6
]

and allows an entity to downgrade its level of confidentiality.
However, our work significantly differs from declassification as it preserves PSNI,
whereas declassification systems
deliberately
release information and thus intentionally
weaken
noninterference.

In particular, robust declassification
[
49
,
53
,
48
,
36
,
16
,
6
]
prevents adversaries from exploiting downgrading of confidentiality, by complementing confidentiality with integrity.
It uses integrity to ensure that downgrading decisions can be trusted, i.e., cannot be influenced by an attacker.
As such, only high-integrity data can influence the taint level to be lowered.
This is similar to our system, where the higher the integrity of a process, the lower level it can regrade the taint level.
The difference, however, is that we enforce extra synchronization policies on our high-integrity processes to ensure that they cannot induce information leaks by lowering the taint level.
This contrasts with work on robust declassification, which introduces leakage intentionally and thus compromises noninterference.

9
Concrete security lattice and security theories

Process configurations and terms are typed relative to a concrete security lattice and a security theory.
A
concrete
lattice
\Psi_{0}
is defined globally for an application and consists of concrete security levels
\iota
.
Our running example lattice

\mathbf{guest}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{alice}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{bank}\qquad\mathbf{guest}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{bob}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{bank}

is an example of a concrete security lattice.
Polymorphic process definitions make use of a
security theory

\Psi
, ranging over security variables
\psi
and
concrete security levels
\iota
from the given concrete security lattice
\Psi_{0}
.
At run-time, all security variables occurring in polymorphic processes will be replaced with concrete security levels.

Definition 4

(Concrete security lattice and security theory)
.

Let
\Psi_{0}\,\triangleq\,\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{E}_{0},\sqcup\rangle
be a concrete join semi-lattice with a partial order
\mathcal{E}_{0}

over concrete security levels
\iota,\eta\in\mathcal{L}
such that
E_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
is of the form
\iota\sqsubseteq\iota^{\prime}
.
We define a security theory
\Psi\,\triangleq\,\langle\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}\rangle

that augments
\Psi_{0}
with security variables
\psi
and relations
\mathcal{E}
over them such that

•

\psi\in\mathcal{V}
is a set of security variables

•

c,d\in\mathcal{S}
is a set of security terms of the security theory
\Psi
, defined by the grammar

c,d:=c\sqcup d\mid\iota\mid\psi

•

E\in\mathcal{E}
is a set of relations over the elements
c,d
of the security theory where
E=c\sqsubseteq d
.

For convenience, we define the projection
\mathcal{E}(\Psi)
to extract the relations
\mathcal{E}
of
\Psi
.
We write

\Psi\Vdash E

if
E
is consequence of the lattice theory based on the relations.
The judgment is defined in
Fig.

16
.

\par\frac{c\sqsubseteq d\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}{\Psi\Vdash c\sqsubseteq d}\par\frac{c\sqsubseteq d\in\mathcal{E}(\Psi)}{\Psi\Vdash c\sqsubseteq d}\par\frac{}{\Psi\Vdash c\sqsubseteq c}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash c_{1}\sqsubseteq c_{2}\quad\Psi\Vdash c_{2}\sqsubseteq c_{3}}{\Psi\Vdash c_{1}\sqsubseteq c_{3}}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash c\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}}{\Psi\Vdash c\sqcup d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}}\par\frac{}{\Psi\Vdash c\sqsubseteq c\sqcup d}\par\frac{}{\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq c\sqcup d}\par

Figure 16:
Inductive definition of
\Psi\Vdash E
.

Process definitions and process spawning rely on an order-preserving substitution, defined as follows:

Definition 5

(Order-preserving substitution)
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a total function that maps security variables to security terms.
Its lifting
\hat{\gamma}
to other syntactic objects, such as security terms
c
, process terms
P
, typing contexts
\Delta
, and security lattices
\Psi
,
is defined by structural induction over the syntactic object, replacing simultaneously all variable occurrences
\psi_{i}
in the object with
\gamma(\psi_{i})
.
The function
\gamma
and its lifting
\hat{\gamma}
must be order-preserving, ensuring that if
\Psi^{\prime}\Vdash E
, then
\hat{\gamma}(\Psi^{\prime})\Vdash\hat{\gamma}(E)
.
To link a spawner and a spawnee, we define an order-preserving substitution
\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}

\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}\,\triangleq\,\text{if}\;\Psi^{\prime}\Vdash E,\text{then}\;\Psi\Vdash\hat{\gamma}(E)

Composition
\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}
of two substitutions
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is defined as usual.
We observe that if both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are order-preserving so is their composition.

The type system requires every spawner to provide an order-preserving substitution
\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}
for the security variables of the spawnee (rule
Spawn
).
As a result, the security theory
\Psi^{\prime}
of the spawnee must be satisfied by the security theory
\Psi
of the spawner,
i.e.,
\text{if}\;\Psi^{\prime}\Vdash E,\text{then}\;\Psi\Vdash\hat{\gamma}(E)
.
Moreover, if the spawner provides a substitution
\delta
for
\Psi_{0}
, i.e.,
\Psi_{0}\Vdash\delta:\Psi
,
so does the spawnee, i.e.,
\Psi_{0}\Vdash\gamma\circ\delta:\Psi^{\prime}
.

10
Regrading policy type system

10.1
Process term typing

The process term typing judgment is of the following form:

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}A\langle c,e\rangle

where
c_{0}
denotes the
running confidentiality
,
e_{0}
the
running integrity
,

c
the
maximal confidentiality
, and
e
the
minimal integrity
.
The running integrity indicates the level of secrets the process is allowed to regrade to
and thus becomes the process’ running confidentiality upon a tail call.
The following presuppositions are imposed on the typing rules:

1.

\Psi\Vdash e_{0}\sqsubseteq c_{0}\sqsubseteq c
and
\Psi\Vdash e_{0}\sqsubseteq e\sqsubseteq c
,

2.

\forall y{:}B\langle d,f\rangle\in\Delta.\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq c,\Psi\Vdash f\sqsubseteq e
.

Moreover, the typing rules for output and input have to conform to the following schema:

1.

Before
sending a message, the running confidentiality and running integrity of the sending process must be

at most
the maximal confidentiality and minimal integrity of the receiving process, and

2.

after
receiving a message, the running confidentiality and running integrity of the receiving process must be
increased to
at least
the maximal confidentiality and minimal integrity of the sending process.

10.1.1
Internal and external choice

\frac{\Psi\Vdash c=e\quad k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;P){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}\oplus\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\oplus_{R_{1}}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash c=e}\quad\forall i,j\in L.\,A_{i}=A_{j}\quad k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;P){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}\oplus\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\oplus_{R_{2}}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle=\langle c,e\rangle\sqcup\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\quad\forall k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta,x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q_{k}@\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle\quad\forall i,j\in L.\Psi\vDash Q_{i}\sim_{\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle}Q_{j}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}\oplus\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{case}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}(\ell{\Rightarrow}Q_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\oplus_{L}$}

\frac{\forall k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P_{k}@\langle c,e\rangle::x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle\quad\forall i,j\in L.\Psi\vDash P_{i}\sim_{\langle c,e\rangle}P_{j}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{case}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}(\ell{\Rightarrow}P_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}\&\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\&_{R}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash c=e\quad\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle c,e\rangle\quad k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta,x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}\&\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\&_{L_{1}}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash c=e}\quad\forall i,j\in L.\,A_{i}=A_{j}\quad\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle c,e\rangle\quad k\in L\quad\Psi;\Delta,x{:}A_{k}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}\&\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\&_{L_{2}}$}

10.1.2
Channel output and input

\frac{\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}B\langle c,e\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,y{:}A\langle d,f\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}A\otimes B\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\otimes_{R}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle=\langle c,e\rangle\sqcup\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\quad\Psi;\Delta,x{:}B\langle c,e\rangle,y{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}A\otimes B\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(y^{\langle c,e\rangle}{\leftarrow}\mathbf{recv}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q_{y^{\langle c,e\rangle}}){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\otimes_{L}$}\par\frac{\Psi;\Delta,y{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c,e\rangle::x{:}B\langle c,e\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}(y^{\langle c,e\rangle}{\leftarrow}\mathbf{recv}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P_{y^{\langle c,e\rangle}}){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}A\multimap B\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\multimap_{R}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle c,e\rangle\quad\Psi;\Delta,x{:}B\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}A\multimap B\langle c,e\rangle,y{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\multimap_{L}$}

10.1.3
Identity

\frac{\Psi\Vdash\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle=\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};y{:}A\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(x^{\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle}\leftarrow y^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}A\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Fwd}$}

10.1.4
Forwarder process

Definition 6
.

For all
Y=A\in\Sigma
, we extend
\Sigma
by adding the following definition to the signature
\Sigma
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\psi_{i}=\psi_{i},\psi_{c}=\psi_{c}\};x^{\prime}:A\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}=\mathtt{Fwd}_{A,{x^{\prime}}^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}\leftarrow{y^{\prime}}^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}}@\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle::y^{\prime}{:}A\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle&\quad Y=A\in\Sigma\end{array}

Here
F_{Y}
is a specific process variable assigned to the forwarder process for type variable
Y
, and
\mathtt{Fwd}_{A,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}
is defined by induction on the structure of
A
as a function from type
A
to process terms as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathtt{Fwd}_{\oplus\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L},y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&\mathbf{case}\,x(\ell\Rightarrow y.\ell;\mathtt{Fwd}_{A_{\ell},y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}})_{\ell\in L}\\[6.0pt]
\mathtt{Fwd}_{\&\{\ell{:}A_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L},y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&\mathbf{case}\,y(\ell\Rightarrow x.\ell;\mathtt{Fwd}_{A_{\ell},y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}})_{\ell\in L}\\[6.0pt]
\mathtt{Fwd}_{A\otimes B,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&w\leftarrow\mathbf{recv}x;\mathbf{send}\,w\,y;\mathtt{Fwd}_{B,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}\\[6.0pt]
\mathtt{Fwd}_{A\multimap B,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&w\leftarrow\mathbf{recv}y;\mathbf{send}\,w\,x;\mathtt{Fwd}_{B,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}\\[6.0pt]
\mathtt{Fwd}_{1,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&\mathbf{wait}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};\mathbf{close}\,y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\\[6.0pt]
\mathtt{Fwd}_{Y;y^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}&:=&F_{Y}[c\mapsto\psi_{c},e\mapsto\psi_{i}][x^{\langle c,e\rangle},y^{\langle c,e\rangle}]\qquad Y=A\in\Sigma\\[6.0pt]
\end{array}

\par\frac{\{\psi_{i}=\psi_{i},\psi_{c}=\psi_{c}\};z:C\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}=\mathtt{Fwd}_{C,w^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}\leftarrow z^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}}@\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle::w{:}C\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle\in\Sigma\quad\gamma(z:C\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{c}\rangle,w:C\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{c}\rangle)=z:C\langle c,e\rangle,y:C\langle c,e\rangle\quad Y=A\in\Sigma\quad\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\psi_{i}=\psi_{i},\psi_{c}=\psi_{c}}{\Psi;z{:}C\langle{c,e}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}[\gamma][x^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle},y^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}]@\langle c,e\rangle::y{:}C\langle{c,e}\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc D-Fwd}$}

Lemma 7
.

Given the extended signature
\Sigma
, for all type
A
, there is a derivation for
x:A\langle{c,e}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}\mathtt{Fwd}_{A,y^{\langle{c,e}\rangle}\leftarrow x^{\langle{c,e}\rangle}}::y:A\langle{c,e}\rangle
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the structure of type
A
. In a base case, where
A
is a type variable
Y
, i.e.,
A=Y
for
Y=C\in\Sigma
, the proof is straightforward by the
D-Fwd
rule since
\{\psi_{i}=\psi_{i},\psi_{c}=\psi_{c}\};z_{1}:C\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}=\mathtt{Fwd}_{C,x^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}\leftarrow z^{\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{1}}@\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle::x_{1}{:}C\langle\psi_{c},\psi_{i}\rangle\in\Sigma
.
The proof of other cases is straightforward.

∎

10.1.5
Spawn

\frac{{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi^{\prime}};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}X=P{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}@}\langle\psi_{0},\omega_{0}\rangle::x{:}A\langle\psi,\omega\rangle\in\Sigma\quad\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}\quad\hat{\gamma}(\Delta_{1}^{\prime})=\Delta_{1}\quad\Psi\Vdash\langle\hat{\gamma}(\psi),\hat{\gamma}(\omega)\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle d,f\rangle\quad\Psi\Vdash f_{0}\sqsubseteq\hat{\gamma}(\psi_{0})\quad\Psi\Vdash f_{0}\sqsubseteq\hat{\gamma}(\omega_{0})\quad\Psi;\Delta_{2},x{:}A\langle\hat{\gamma}(\psi),\hat{\gamma}(\omega)\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash_{\Sigma}((x^{\langle\hat{\gamma}(\psi),\hat{\gamma}(\omega)\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta_{1})@\langle\hat{\gamma}(\psi_{0}),\hat{\gamma}(\omega_{0})\rangle;Q_{x}){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Spawn}$}\par

Rule
Spawn
relies on an order-preserving substitution
\Psi\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}
,
guaranteeing that the security terms provided by the spawner comply with the order expected among those terms by the spawnee.
Rule
Spawn
moreover establishes the above invariants for the newly spawned process,

\Psi\Vdash\langle\hat{\gamma}(\psi),\hat{\gamma}(\omega)\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle d,f\rangle
, and
allows the newly spawned process to start at least at the spawner’s running integrity
f_{0}

for running confidentiality and integrity, facilitating regrading to
f_{0}
in case of a tail call.
Note that
\Psi\Vdash f_{0}\sqsubseteq\hat{\gamma}(\omega_{0})
prevents the spawnee from employing more patterns checks
than the spawner because the spawnee would otherwise be affected by the spawners negligence.

10.1.6
Termination

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\cdot\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{close}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}){@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle}::x{:}\mathbf{1}\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathbf{1}_{R}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle=\langle c,e\rangle\sqcup\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}Q@\langle d_{1},f_{1}\rangle::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}\mathbf{1}\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{wait}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mathbf{1}_{L}$}

10.1.7
Silent unfolding

We adopt an
equi-recursive

[
20
]
interpretation of recursive session type definitions, avoiding explicit (un)fold messages.
The below rules allow silent unfolding of a type definition.

\frac{Y=A\in\Sigma\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}A\langle c,e\rangle}{\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle::x{:}Y\langle c,e\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc TVar}_{R}$}\par\frac{Y=A\in\Sigma\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}A\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};\Delta,x{:}Y\langle c,e\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}(Q){@\langle d_{0},f_{0}\rangle}::z{:}C\langle d,f\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc TVar}_{L}$}

10.1.8
Signature checking

The signature
\Sigma
collects process definitions (rule
\Sigma_{3}
) and possibly recursive session type definitions (rule
\Sigma_{2}
).
The judgment
\Vdash_{\Sigma}A\;\mathbf{wfmd}
denotes a well-formed session type definition, which, if recursive,
must be
equi-recursive

[
20
]
and
contractive
.
Equi-recursiveness avoids explicit (un)fold messages and relates types up to their unfolding.
Contractiveness demands an exchange before recurring.
Signature checking happens relative to a globally fixed security lattice
\Psi_{0}
of concrete security levels
and relative to the entire signature
\Sigma
.

\frac{}{\Vdash_{\Sigma;{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi_{0}}}(\cdot)\;\mathbf{sig}}\quad\textsc{right=$\Sigma_{1}$}\par\frac{\Vdash_{\Sigma}A\;\mathbf{wfmd}\quad\Vdash_{\Sigma;{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi_{0}}}\Sigma^{\prime}\;\mathbf{sig}}{\Vdash_{\Sigma;{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi_{0}}}Y=A,\Sigma^{\prime}\;\mathbf{sig}}\quad\textsc{right=$\Sigma_{2}$}\par\frac{\forall i\in\{1\dots n\}.\Psi\Vdash\langle\psi_{i},\omega_{i}\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle\psi,\omega\rangle,\Psi\Vdash\omega_{i}\sqsubseteq\psi_{i}\quad\Psi\Vdash\langle\psi_{0},\omega_{0}\rangle\sqsubseteq\langle\psi,\omega\rangle\quad\Psi\Vdash\omega_{0}\sqsubseteq\psi_{0}\quad\Psi\Vdash\omega\sqsubseteq\psi\quad\Psi;y_{1}{:}B_{1}\langle\psi_{1},\omega_{1}\rangle,\dots,y_{n}{:}B_{n}\langle\psi_{n},\omega_{n}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}P@\langle\psi_{0},\omega_{0}\rangle::x{:}A\langle\psi,\omega\rangle\quad\Vdash_{\Sigma;{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi_{0}}}\Sigma^{\prime}\;\mathbf{sig}}{\Vdash_{\Sigma;{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi_{0}}}{\it\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Psi};y_{1}{:}B_{1}\langle\psi_{1},\omega_{1}\rangle,\dots,y_{n}{:}B_{n}\langle\psi_{n},\omega_{n}\rangle\vdash_{\Sigma}X=P@\langle\psi_{0},\omega_{0}\rangle::x{:}A\langle\psi,\omega\rangle,\Sigma^{\prime}\;\mathbf{sig}}\quad\textsc{right=$\Sigma_{3}$}

10.2
Synchronization patterns

The judgment is of the form
\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q
, and the rules are defined inductively below.
The judgment states that process terms
P
and
Q
are
synchronized
in terms of their communication pattern,
meaning that if
P
outputs along channel
x
, so must
Q
, and that if
P
inputs along channel
x
, so must
Q
, and vice versa,
and is pairwise called for all branches of a case statement.
The check is
conditioned
on
the confidentiality
d
and running integrity
f
of the recipient after branching.
The idea is to rule out leakage of the newly learned secret of level
d
by the presence of
different communication patterns in the recipient’s continuation
as long as the recipient may actually regrade to the level of the secret.
The confidentiality of the learned secret
d
remains constant under inductive invocations,
the running integrity of the recipient
f
is updated to the recipient’s running integrity after a receive.

The inductive definition below distinguishes the following cases:

•

If
\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f
, then
\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q
are trivially related
because the learned secret is not greater than or equal to the level to which the recipient regrades.
This case is a base case of the inductive definition.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}
, the rules consider whether the next action is a close, send (except close), or receive:

–

In case of a close, the rules insist that they are synchronized.
This is the other base case of the inductive definition.

–

In case of a receive, the receives are checked to be synchronized and the pattern check is invoked inductively on the continuation,
with updated running integrity (
f\sqcup e
).

–

In case of a send, the sends must only by synchronized if
the carrier channel’s minimal integrity
e
is not greater than or equal to the level
d
of the secret (
d\not\sqsubseteq e
).
In either case, the pattern check is invoked inductively on the continuation, with unchanged running integrity.

For convenience, we define a process term and a message term with an output prefix as follows:

\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.P\,\triangleq\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;P\,\mid\,\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P

\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.\_\,\triangleq\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k\,\mid\,\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}

10.2.1
Unsynchronized rules

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Unsync}_{1}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.Q}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Unsync}_{2}$}\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}{\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-Unsync}_{3}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e_{0}\quad\forall y{:}B\langle c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle\in\Delta.\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e^{\prime}\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta)@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle;P_{x}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Unsync-Spawn}_{1}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e_{0}\quad\forall y{:}B\langle c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle\in\Delta.\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e^{\prime}\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}(x^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta)@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle;Q_{x}}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Unsync-Spawn}_{2}$}

10.2.2
Internal and external choice

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e}\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k;P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.\ell;Q}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-SndLab}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\forall j\in I,k\in L.\Psi\vDash P_{j}\sim_{\langle d,f\sqcup e\rangle}Q_{k}}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{case}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}(\ell{\Rightarrow}P_{\ell})_{\ell\in I}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{case}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}(\ell{\Rightarrow}Q_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-RcvLab}$}

10.2.3
Channel output and input

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e}\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{send}\,y\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-SndChn}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\vDash[y/y_{1}]P\sim_{\langle d,f\sqcup e\rangle}[y/y_{2}]Q}{\Psi\vDash y_{1}{\leftarrow}\mathbf{recv}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P_{y_{1}}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}y_{2}{\leftarrow}\mathbf{recv}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q_{y_{2}}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-RcvChn}$}

10.2.4
spawn

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{(\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e_{0}}\;\mathsf{or}\;\exists y{:}B\langle c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle\in\Delta.\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e^{\prime}})}\quad\Psi\vDash[x/x_{1}]P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}[x/x_{2}]Q}{\Psi\vDash(x_{1}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta)@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle;P_{x_{1}}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}(x_{2}^{\langle c,e\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta)@\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle;Q_{x_{2}}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-Spawn}$}

10.2.5
identity

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}}{\Psi\vDash x^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}\leftarrow y^{\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}x^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}\leftarrow y^{\langle c_{2},e_{2}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-Fwd}$}

10.2.6
Forwarder process

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}}{\Psi\vDash F_{Y}[\gamma][x^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle},y^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}]\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}F_{Y}[\gamma][x^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle},y^{\langle c_{1},e_{1}\rangle}]}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-D-Fwd}$}

\par\frac{\psi=\psi;z_{1}:C[\psi]\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}=\mathtt{Fwd}_{C,x^{\psi}_{1}\leftarrow z^{\psi}_{1}}@\psi::x_{1}{:}C[\psi]\in\Sigma\quad Y=A\in\Sigma\quad\Psi\Vdash\gamma_{\mathsf{sec}}:\psi=\psi\quad z{:}C[c],y{:}C[c]\Vdash(\gamma_{\mathsf{sec}},\gamma_{\mathsf{var}})::z_{1}{:}C[\psi],x_{1}{:}C[\psi]}{\Psi;z{:}C[c]\vdash_{\Sigma}F_{Y}[(\gamma_{\mathsf{sec}},\gamma_{\mathsf{var}})]@c::y{:}C[c]}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc D-Fwd}$}

10.2.7
Termination

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{close}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{close}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Close}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\vDash P\sim_{\langle d,f\sqcup e\rangle}Q}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{wait}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};P\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{wait}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle};Q}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-Wait}$}

10.2.8
Runtime - messages

\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e\quad\Psi\vDash\_\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}M}{\Psi\vDash\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.\_\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}M}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-Unsync}_{1}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e\quad\Psi\vDash M\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\_}{\Psi\vDash M\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\uparrow_{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}.\_}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-Unsync}_{2}$}\par\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e}{\Psi\vDash\_\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\_}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-done}_{2}$}\par\par\frac{\Psi\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}{\Psi\vDash M\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}M^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-Unsync}_{3}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e}}{\Psi\vDash x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.k\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}x^{\langle c,e\rangle}.\ell}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-SndLab}$}\par\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}\quad\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq e}}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{send}\,y\,{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{send}\,y\,{x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-SndChn}$}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0.6719,0.2617,0.2422}{\Psi\not\Vdash d\sqsubseteq f}}{\Psi\vDash\mathbf{close}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}\sim_{\langle d,f\rangle}\mathbf{close}\,x^{\langle c,e\rangle}}\quad\textsc{$\mbox{\sc Brb-M-Close}$}\par\par

10.3
Asynchronous dynamics

We define an asynchronous dynamics for
\mathsf{SINTEGRITY}
that we adopt from prior work
[
8
,
21
,
23
]
and extend to account for polymorphic process definitions.
The dynamics is given in
Fig.

17
and uses multiset rewriting rules
[
15
]
.
Multiset rewriting rules express the dynamics as state transitions between configurations and are
local
in that they only mention the parts of a configuration they rewrite.

D-Fwd

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,F_{Y}[\gamma][x_{\beta}^{\langle p\rangle},y_{\alpha}^{\langle p\rangle}]@p){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}

(p_{\psi}=p_{\psi};x:{C}\langle p_{\psi}\rangle\vdash F_{y}=\mathtt{Fwd}_{C,y^{p_{\psi}}\leftarrow x^{p_{\psi}}}@p_{\psi}::y:C\langle p_{\psi}\rangle\in\Sigma)

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,\mathtt{Fwd}_{C;y_{\alpha}^{\langle p\rangle}\leftarrow x_{\beta}^{\langle p\rangle}}@p)

(x\langle p_{\psi}\rangle,y\langle p_{\psi}\rangle\Vdash\gamma:x_{\beta}\langle p\rangle,y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle)

Spawn

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(x^{\langle p^{\prime}\rangle}\leftarrow X[\gamma]\leftarrow\Delta)@\langle p_{2}\rangle;Q@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}

(\Psi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash X=P@{\langle\psi_{0},\omega_{0}\rangle}::x:B^{\prime}\langle\psi,\omega\rangle\in\Sigma)

\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbf{proc}(x_{0}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,([x_{0},\Delta/x,\Delta^{\prime}]\hat{\gamma}(P))@\langle p_{2}\rangle)\;\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,([x_{0}/x]Q)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)

(\Psi_{0}\Vdash\gamma:\Psi^{\prime},\,\,x_{0}\,\mathit{fresh})

1_{\mathsf{snd}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(\mathbf{close}\,y_{\alpha})@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{close}\,y_{\alpha})

1_{\mathsf{rcv}}

\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{close}\,y_{\alpha})\;\mathbf{proc}(x_{\beta}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,(\mathbf{wait}\,y_{\alpha};Q)@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\mathbf{proc}(x_{\beta}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,Q@\langle p_{1}\rangle\sqcup\langle p\rangle)

\oplus_{\mathsf{snd}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,y_{\alpha}.k;P@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha+1}\langle p\rangle,([y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)\;\mathbf{msg}(y_{\alpha}.k;y_{\alpha}\leftarrow y_{\alpha+1})

\oplus_{\mathsf{rcv}}

\mathbf{msg}(y_{\alpha}.k)\;\mathbf{proc}(u_{\gamma}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,\mathbf{case}\,y_{\alpha}^{\langle p\rangle}((\ell\Rightarrow P_{\ell})_{\ell\in L})@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\,\mathbf{proc}(u_{\gamma}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,([y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P_{k})@\langle p_{1}\rangle\sqcup\langle p\rangle)

\&_{\mathsf{snd}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(x_{\beta}.k;P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\mathbf{msg}(x_{\beta}.k)\;\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,([x_{\beta+1}/x_{\beta}]P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)

\&_{\mathsf{rcv}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(\mathbf{case}\,y_{\alpha}(\ell\Rightarrow P_{\ell})_{\ell\in L})@\langle p_{1}\rangle)\;\mathbf{msg}(y_{\alpha}.k){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\,\mathbf{proc}(v_{\delta}\langle p\rangle,([y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P_{k})@\langle p\rangle)

\otimes_{\mathsf{snd}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,y_{\alpha};P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\,\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha+1}\langle p\rangle,([y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)\;\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,y_{\alpha})

\otimes_{\mathsf{rcv}}

\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,y_{\alpha})\;\mathbf{proc}(u_{\gamma}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,(w\leftarrow\mathbf{recv}\,y^{\langle p\rangle}_{\alpha};P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\mathbf{proc}(u_{\gamma}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle,([x_{\beta}/w][y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P)@(\langle p_{1}\rangle\sqcup\langle p\rangle))

\multimap_{\mathsf{snd}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,u_{\gamma};P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,u_{\gamma})\;\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,([u_{\gamma+1}/u_{\gamma}]P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)

\multimap_{\mathsf{rcv}}

\mathbf{proc}(y_{\alpha}\langle p\rangle,(w\leftarrow\mathbf{recv}\,y_{\alpha};P)@\langle p_{1}\rangle)\;\mathbf{msg}(\mathbf{send}\,x_{\beta}\,y_{\alpha}){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\;\;\mapsto\;\;}\,\mathbf{proc}(v_{\delta}\langle p\rangle,([x_{\beta}/w][y_{\alpha+1}/y_{\alpha}]P)@\langle p\rangle)
